- Notes - Water glass: - Soluble sodium silicate (\( Na_2SiO_3 \)), from sodium carbonate + silica. - Safety glass: - Vinyl acetate resin between glass layers. - Optical fibre: - For telecommunication, endoscopy. - Ground glass: - Soda glass ground with emery/turpentine. - Glass wool: - Fireproof, insulating, stronger than steel, used in fibre glass, glass-reinforced plastic. - Cement - Definition: - Calcium aluminium silicate, grey powder, hardens with water (exothermic). - Origin: - Introduced by Joseph Aspdin (1824, England), resembles Portland limestone. - Raw Materials: - Limestone (\( CaO \)), clay (silica, alumina, ferric oxide), gypsum (2–3%). - Composition: - \( CaO (50–60%), SiO_2 (20–25%), Al_2O_3 (5–10%), MgO (2–3%), Fe_2O_3 (1–2%), SO_3 (1–2%) \). - Manufacture: - Limestone + clay heated to form clinker, mixed with gypsum. - Notes - Gypsum slows setting, ensures hardening. - Water sprinkled to cool exothermic setting. - Excess lime causes cracks; excess alumina speeds solidification. - Fertilizers - Definition: - Supply nutrients (N, P, K) to enhance soil fertility, plant growth. - Types - Nitrogenous: - From ammonia (e.g., ammonium sulphate (\( (NH_4)_2SO_4 \), 25% N), calcium ammonium nitrate (\( Ca(NO_3)_2 \cdot NH_4NO_3 \), 20% N), urea (\( NH_2CONH_2 \), 46% N, pH-neutral)). - Phosphatic: - Superphosphate of lime (\( Ca(H_2PO_4)_2 + \) gypsum, 16–20% \( P_2O_5 \)), triple superphosphate, Thomas slag (14–18% \( P_2O_5 \)), potassium chloride/nitrate/sulphate. - NP: - Dihydrogen ammoniated phosphate (supplies N, P). - NPK: - Mixed fertilizers (supply N, P, K). - Notes - Ammonium sulphate: - Produced at Sindri, Jharkhand; nitrates absorbed by plants. - Calcium ammonium nitrate: - Nagal, Punjab; highly soluble, no soil impact. - Urea: - From \( CO_2 + NH_3 \) at 125–150°C, 8.5 atm, applied 3–4 days before watering. - Nitrolim: - \( Ca(CN)_2 + C \), used before seeding. - Calcium nitrate: - Norwegian saltpeter. - Explosives - Definition: - Produce heat, energy, and sound on combustion, consist of fuel + oxidizer or pure compounds. - Types - Trinitroglycerine (TNG, Noble’s oil): - Ester from glycerine + \( H_2SO_4 + HNO_3 \), colorless, oily, used in dynamite (invented 1846). - Trinitrotoluene (TNT): - From toluene + nitrating mixture (\( H_2SO_4 + HNO_3 \)), invented 1863, used by UK. - RDX (Cyclotrimethylene trinitramine): - Cyclonite/hexogen/T4, invented by Henning (1899), 1510 kcal explosion energy, used in plastic explosives (C-4 with Al powder). - Trinitrophenol (TNP, Picric acid): - From phenol + \( H_2SO_4 + HNO_3 \), ultra-explosive. - Dynamite: - Invented by Alfred Nobel (1863), 3 parts nitroglycerin + 1 part diatomaceous earth + sodium carbonate, used in mining. - Notes - Gunpowder: - Nitrate + sulphur + charcoal. - Gelatin dynamite: - Contains nitrocellulose. - Modern dynamite: - Uses sodium/ammonium nitrate + wood pulp.
- Chemistry PYQs - Q1: Which one of the following can be used to confirm whether drinking water contains a gamma emitting isotope or not? - Options: - (a) Microscope - (b) Lead plate - (c) Scintillation counter - (d) Spectrophotometer - Answer: - (c) Scintillation counter - Analysis: - Physical and chemical principles: - Gamma rays are high-energy photons ($$ E = h\nu $$, typically >100 keV) from nuclear transitions (e.g., Cs-137: $$ \gamma \approx 662 \, \text{keV} $$). - Scintillation counters use scintillators (e.g., NaI(Tl)) to convert gamma energy into light via electron excitation, detected by a photomultiplier tube: $$ \gamma + \text{NaI(Tl)} \to e^- \to \text{light} $$. - Quantitative aspect: Detection sensitivity ~0.1 Bq/L, aligning with WHO guidelines for water safety (~1 Bq/L for Cs-137). - Why (c) is correct: - Scintillation counters provide high-resolution energy spectra, identifying specific isotopes by their gamma signatures. - Why not others: - (a) Microscopes resolve visible structures (~200 nm), far below gamma wavelengths (~10⁻¹² m). - (b) Lead plates shield gamma rays ($$ \mu \approx 0.1 \, \text{cm}^{-1} $$ at 1 MeV) but cannot detect or confirm isotopes. - (d) Spectrophotometers measure UV-Vis absorption (~200–800 nm), irrelevant for gamma rays. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Scintillation detectors developed in the 1940s, refined post-WWII for nuclear monitoring. - Example: Post-Fukushima, scintillation counters detected Cs-137 in water at ~0.5 Bq/L, ensuring safety. - Applications: Environmental monitoring, nuclear safety, medical diagnostics. - Experimental context: Calibrated using standard sources (e.g., Co-60) to measure energy resolution and background radiation. - Q2: Which one of the following pairs of materials serves as electrodes in chargeable batteries commonly used in devices such as torchlights, electric shavers, etc.? - Options: - (a) Nickel and cadmium - (b) Zinc and carbon - (c) Lead peroxide and lead - (d) Iron and cadmium - Answer: - (a) Nickel and cadmium - Analysis: - Chemical principles: - Nickel-cadmium (NiCd) batteries use nickel oxyhydroxide ($$ \text{NiOOH} $$) as the cathode and cadmium ($$ \text{Cd} $$) as the anode. Reaction: $$ \text{Cd} + \text{NiOOH} + 2\text{H}_2\text{O} \leftrightarrow \text{Cd(OH)}_2 + \text{Ni(OH)}_2 $$, $$ E^\circ \approx 1.2 \, \text{V} $$. - Quantitative aspect: NiCd delivers ~1000 charge cycles, high discharge rates (~10C), and energy density ~40–60 Wh/kg. - Why (a) is correct: - NiCd batteries were standard for portable devices due to durability and rechargeability. - Why not others: - (b) Zinc-carbon batteries are primary (non-rechargeable), using $$ \text{Zn} \to \text{Zn}^{2+} + 2e^- $$, unsuitable for recharge. - (c) Lead peroxide ($$ \text{PbO}_2 $$) and lead are used in lead-acid batteries, bulky for cars, not portable devices. - (d) Iron-cadmium is not a standard battery chemistry; iron is used in niche NiFe batteries. - Relevant information: - Historical context: NiCd batteries commercialized in the 1940s, phased out in the EU (2006) due to cadmium’s toxicity (RoHS directive). - Example: A AA NiCd battery (1.2 V, 600 mAh) powers a torchlight for ~5 hours at 100 mA. - Applications: Portable electronics, emergency lighting (now largely replaced by NiMH/Li-ion). - Experimental context: Battery performance tested via cyclic voltammetry and charge-discharge cycles. - Q3: 'Yellow cake', an item of smuggling across borders, is: - Options: - (a) a crude form of heroin - (b) a crude form of cocaine - (c) uranium oxide - (d) unrefined gold - Answer: - (c) uranium oxide - Analysis: - Chemical and nuclear principles: - Yellow cake is $$ \text{U}_3\text{O}_8 $$, a uranium concentrate from ore milling, containing ~80% uranium, used for nuclear fuel after conversion to $$ \text{UF}_6 $$ and enrichment (U-235 from ~0.7% to 3–5%). - Quantitative aspect: U-235’s thermal neutron fission cross-section (~584 barns) makes yellow cake critical for reactors. - Why (c) is correct: - $$ \text{U}_3\text{O}_8 $$ is a key nuclear material, valued in smuggling for proliferation risks. - Why not others: - (a), (b) Heroin and cocaine are organic drugs, unrelated to nuclear chemistry. - (d) Unrefined gold lacks nuclear significance, though smuggled for economic reasons. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Yellow cake production scaled post-WWII for nuclear programs; monitored by IAEA since the 1960s. - Example: A ton of yellow cake yields ~7–10 kg enriched uranium for reactors. - Applications: Nuclear fuel cycle, energy production. - Experimental context: Uranium content analyzed via X-ray fluorescence or mass spectrometry. - Q4: The difference between a nuclear reactor and an atomic bomb is that: - Options: - (a) no chain reaction takes place in nuclear reactor while in the atomic bomb there is a chain reaction - (b) the chain reaction in nuclear reactor is controlled - (c) the chain reaction in nuclear reactor is not controlled - (d) no chain reaction takes place in atomic bomb while it takes place in nuclear reactor - Answer: - (b) the chain reaction in nuclear reactor is controlled - Analysis: - Nuclear physics principles: - Both systems use fission ($$ ^{235}\text{U} + n \to \text{fission products} + 2–3n + \sim 200 \, \text{MeV} $$). Reactors maintain criticality ($$ k = 1 $$) via control rods (e.g., boron, $$ \sigma_{\text{abs}} \approx 760 \, \text{barns} $$); bombs achieve supercriticality ($$ k > 1 $$) for exponential neutron growth. - Quantitative aspect: Reactor power ~1–3 GW (thermal), controlled to $$ k \approx 1 $$; bombs release ~10¹⁷ J in microseconds. - Why (b) is correct: - Reactors use moderators (e.g., graphite) and control rods to regulate neutron flux, ensuring steady power output. - Why not others: - (a), (d) Incorrect: Reactors sustain controlled chain reactions; bombs rely on uncontrolled ones. - (c) Incorrect: Control is a hallmark of reactor design. - Relevant information: - Historical context: First reactor (CP-1, 1942) by Fermi; first bomb (Trinity, 1945). - Example: A 1 GW reactor requires ~3 kg U-235/day; a bomb uses ~15 kg for ~20 kt yield. - Applications: Nuclear power, weaponry. - Experimental context: Criticality tested via neutron multiplication measurements. - Q5: The alpha particle carries two positive charges. Its mass is very nearly equal to that of: - Options: - (a) two protons - (b) an atom of helium - (c) sum of masses of two positrons and two neutrons - (d) two positrons as each positron carries a single positive charge - Answer: - (b) an atom of helium - Analysis: - Nuclear chemistry principles: - Alpha particle ($$ ^4_2\text{He} $$): 2 protons, 2 neutrons, mass ~4.0015 u, charge +2e. Helium atom ($$ ^4\text{He} $$): mass ~4.0026 u (includes 2 electrons, ~0.0011 u). - Quantitative aspect: Proton mass ~1.0078 u; neutron ~1.0087 u; positron ~0.00055 u. - Why (b) is correct: - Alpha particle mass is nearly identical to helium-4 atom, differing only by electron mass. - Why not others: - (a) Two protons: ~2.016 u, too light. - (c) Two positrons (~0.0011 u) + two neutrons (~2.017 u) ≈ 2.018 u, too light. - (d) Positrons contribute negligible mass (~0.0011 u). - Relevant information: - Historical context: Alpha particles identified by Rutherford (1899) as helium nuclei. - Example: Am-241 in smoke detectors emits alphas (~5.5 MeV). - Applications: Radiation therapy, nuclear research. - Experimental context: Alpha mass measured via mass spectrometry. - Q6: Match the names of outstanding Indian scientists given in List I with area of their specialized work given in List II and select the correct answer by using the codes given below the lists: - List-I: - A. Dr. Raja Ramanna - B. Dr. M.S. Swaminathan - C. Prof. U.R. Rao - D. Prof. Meghnad Saha - List-II: - 1. Plant chemistry - 2. Nuclear physics - 3. Thermodynamics & astrophysics - 4. Space research - 5. Agricultural science - Options: - (a) A-3, B-5, C-2, D-1 - (b) A-2, B-1, C-4, D-3 - (c) A-2, B-5, C-4, D-3 - (d) A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2 - Answer: - (c) A-2, B-5, C-4, D-3 - Analysis: - Scientific contributions: - A. Raja Ramanna: Nuclear physics, led India’s 1974 nuclear test (Pokhran-I). - B. M.S. Swaminathan: Agricultural science, pioneered Green Revolution via high-yield wheat/rice varieties. - C. U.R. Rao: Space research, developed ISRO’s satellite program (e.g., Aryabhata, 1975). - D. Meghnad Saha: Thermodynamics & astrophysics, formulated Saha ionization equation ($$ \frac{n_{i+1}}{n_i} \propto T^{3/2} e^{-\frac{E_i}{kT}} $$) for stellar atmospheres. - Why (c) is correct: - Matches each scientist’s primary field accurately. - Why not others: - (a), (b), (d) Mismatch fields (e.g., Ramanna not thermodynamics, Swaminathan not plant chemistry). - Relevant information: - Historical context: Saha’s work (1920s) foundational for astrophysics; Ramanna’s contributions shaped India’s nuclear policy. - Example: Saha’s equation predicts ionization in stars at ~10⁴ K. - Applications: Nuclear energy, agriculture, space technology, stellar spectroscopy. - Experimental context: Saha’s equation validated via stellar spectra analysis. - Q7: Which one of the following elements is essential for the construction of nuclear reactors? - Options: - (a) Cobalt - (b) Nickel - (c) Zirconium - (d) Tungsten - Answer: - (c) Zirconium - Analysis: - Chemical and nuclear principles: - Zirconium (Zr-90, ~51% abundance) is used in fuel rod cladding due to low neutron capture cross-section (~0.18 barns) and corrosion resistance in high-temperature water (~350°C). - Quantitative aspect: Zr’s melting point (~1855°C) and thermal conductivity (~22 W/m·K) suit reactor conditions. - Why (c) is correct: - Zirconium alloys (e.g., Zircaloy) encase UO₂ fuel, minimizing neutron loss and ensuring structural integrity. - Why not others: - (a) Cobalt-59 absorbs neutrons (~37 barns), used in control rods, not structural components. - (b) Nickel is used in alloys but not critical for reactors. - (d) Tungsten’s high melting point (~3422°C) is useful elsewhere, not reactors. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Zirconium adopted in reactors post-1950s for its nuclear properties. - Example: Zircaloy-2 contains ~1.5% Sn, enhancing corrosion resistance. - Applications: Pressurized water reactors (PWRs), boiling water reactors (BWRs). - Experimental context: Neutron cross-sections measured via reactor irradiation tests. - Q8: Barium in a suitable form is administered to patients before an X-ray examination of the stomach, because: - Options: - (a) barium allows X-rays to pass through the stomach on account of its transparency to X-rays - (b) barium is a good absorber of γ-rays and this helps the stomach to appear clearly in contrast with the other regions in the picture - (c) barium is a good absorber of X-rays and this helps the stomach to appear clearly in contrast with the other regions in the picture - (d) barium salts are white in colour and this helps the stomach to appear clearly in contrast with other regions in the pictures - Answer: - (c) barium is a good absorber of X-rays and this helps the stomach to appear clearly in contrast with the other regions in the picture - Analysis: - Chemical and imaging principles: - Barium sulfate ($$ \text{BaSO}_4 $$) is used due to barium’s high atomic number ($$ Z = 56 $$), enhancing X-ray absorption ($$ \mu \propto Z^4/E^3 $$, $$ E \approx 50–100 \, \text{keV} $$). - Quantitative aspect: $$ \text{BaSO}_4 $$ mass attenuation coefficient ~5 cm²/g at 100 keV, providing high contrast. - Why (c) is correct: - Barium’s opacity to X-rays highlights the stomach against softer tissues ($$ \mu_{\text{tissue}} \approx 0.2 \, \text{cm}^2/\text{g} $$). - Why not others: - (a) Barium is opaque, not transparent, to X-rays. - (b) Medical X-rays (~50–100 keV), not gamma rays (~>100 keV), are used. - (d) Color is irrelevant; X-ray imaging depends on absorption. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Barium sulfate introduced in radiology in the early 20th century. - Example: A 500 mL $$ \text{BaSO}_4 $$ suspension (40% w/v) ensures clear GI tract imaging. - Applications: Diagnostic radiology, gastroenterology. - Experimental context: Contrast quality tested via phantom studies in X-ray systems. - Q9: Cobalt-60 is commonly used in radiation therapy because it emits: - Options: - (a) alpha rays - (b) beta rays - (c) gamma rays - (d) X-rays - Answer: - (c) gamma rays - Analysis: - Nuclear chemistry principles: - Cobalt-60 ($$ ^{60}\text{Co} $$) decays via beta emission to $$ ^{60}\text{Ni} $$, which emits gamma rays (~1.17, 1.33 MeV): $$ ^{60}\text{Co} \to ^{60}\text{Ni} + \beta^- + \gamma $$. - Quantitative aspect: Half-life ~5.27 years, gamma energy ideal for deep-tissue penetration. - Why (c) is correct: - Gamma rays deliver precise radiation doses in teletherapy (e.g., Gamma Knife). - Why not others: - (a) Alpha rays (~4–9 MeV) have low penetration (~mm in tissue). - (b) Beta rays (~0.3 MeV for Co-60) are less penetrating (~cm). - (d) X-rays are produced via electron bombardment, not nuclear decay. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Co-60 therapy pioneered in the 1950s for cancer treatment. - Example: A 1 TBq Co-60 source delivers ~1 Gy/min at 1 m. - Applications: Oncology, sterilization. - Experimental context: Gamma dosimetry uses ionization chambers for calibration. - Q10: Assertion (A): Large cold storage plants use ammonia as refrigerant while domestic refrigerators use chlorofluorocarbons. - Reason (R): Ammonia can be liquefied at ambient temperature and low pressure. - Options: - (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A - (b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct explanation of A - (c) A is true but R is false - (d) A is false but R is true - Answer: - (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A - Analysis: - Chemical and thermodynamic principles: - Assertion: Ammonia ($$ \text{NH}_3 $$) is used in industrial refrigeration due to high latent heat of vaporization (~1370 kJ/kg). Domestic refrigerators historically used CFCs (e.g., R-12), now HFCs (e.g., R-134a) due to ozone depletion concerns (Montreal Protocol, 1987). - Reason: Ammonia liquefies at ~25°C under ~8–10 bar, suitable for industrial systems. Critical temperature: 132.4°C. - Quantitative aspect: Ammonia’s coefficient of performance (COP) ~4–5, higher than HFCs (~3–4). - Why (a) is correct: - Ammonia’s liquefaction properties enable efficient industrial refrigeration, explaining its use over CFCs/HFCs in large plants. - Why not others: - (b) Incorrect: Liquefaction is a valid explanation. - (c) R is true. - (d) A is true, though HFCs now dominate domestic use. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Ammonia refrigeration dates to the 1850s; CFCs phased out post-1987. - Example: A 100 kW ammonia system cools ~50 tons of storage at -20°C. - Applications: Cold storage, food processing. - Experimental context: Refrigerant efficiency tested via vapor-compression cycle analysis. - Q11: Most of the explosions in mines occur due to the mixing of: - Options: - (a) hydrogen with oxygen - (b) oxygen with acetylene - (c) methane with air - (d) carbon dioxide with ethane - Answer: - (c) methane with air - Analysis: - Combustion chemistry principles: - Methane ($$ \text{CH}_4 $$) in air (5–15% concentration) is explosive: $$ \text{CH}_4 + 2\text{O}_2 \to \text{CO}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O} $$, $$ \Delta H \approx -891 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$. - Quantitative aspect: Lower explosive limit (LEL) ~5%, upper ~15%, common in coal mines. - Why (c) is correct: - Methane, a byproduct of coal mining, forms explosive mixtures with air. - Why not others: - (a) Hydrogen-oxygen mixtures are explosive but less common in mines. - (b) Acetylene is used in welding, not a mine gas. - (d) CO₂ is non-flammable; ethane is rare in mines. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Mine explosions (e.g., 1906 Courrières disaster) drove methane safety research. - Example: Methane at 7% in air can ignite with a spark (~0.3 mJ). - Applications: Mine ventilation, gas sensors. - Experimental context: Explosive limits tested in combustion chambers. - Q12: In an atom, the order of filling up of the orbitals is governed by: - Options: - (a) Aufbau’s principle - (b) Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle - (c) Hund’s rule - (d) Pauli’s exclusion principle - Answer: - (a) Aufbau’s principle - Analysis: - Quantum chemistry principles: - Aufbau’s principle: Electrons fill orbitals in order of increasing energy ($$ E \approx -\frac{13.6}{n^2} \, \text{eV} $$ for H, adjusted by $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$ in multi-electron atoms). - Example: Filling order: 1s → 2s → 2p → 3s → 3p → 4s. - Quantitative aspect: Energy differences (e.g., 4s vs. 3d) depend on screening effects. - Why (a) is correct: - Aufbau governs electron configuration stability (e.g., Ne: $$ 1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 $$). - Why not others: - (b) Heisenberg’s principle ($$ \Delta x \cdot \Delta p \geq \hbar/2 $$) addresses measurement limits. - (c) Hund’s rule maximizes unpaired electrons in degenerate orbitals. - (d) Pauli’s exclusion principle limits orbitals to two electrons ($$ m_s = \pm 1/2 $$). - Relevant information: - Historical context: Aufbau formalized in the 1920s with quantum mechanics. - Example: Cr ([Ar] 4s¹ 3d⁵) deviates due to half-filled stability. - Applications: Chemical bonding, spectroscopy. - Experimental context: Electron configurations verified via atomic emission spectra. - Q13: A radioactive substance has a half-life of four months. Three-fourths of the substance would decay in: - Options: - (a) 3 months - (b) 4 months - (c) 8 months - (d) 12 months - Answer: - (c) 8 months - Analysis: - Radioactive decay principles: - Decay follows $$ N = N_0 \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{t/T} $$, where $$ T = 4 \, \text{months} $$. For 3/4 decay, $$ N = N_0/4 $$: - $$ \frac{1}{4} = \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{t/4} $$, so $$ \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^2 = \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{t/4} $$, $$ t/4 = 2 $$, $$ t = 8 \, \text{months} $$. - Quantitative aspect: Decay constant $$ \lambda = \frac{\ln 2}{T} \approx 0.173 \, \text{month}^{-1} $$. - Why (c) is correct: - Two half-lives (8 months) reduce the substance to 25% ($$ N_0/4 $$). - Why not others: - (a) 3 months: ~35% decay ($$ N \approx 0.65 N_0 $$). - (b) 4 months: 50% decay ($$ N = N_0/2 $$). - (d) 12 months: ~87.5% decay ($$ N \approx 0.125 N_0 $$). - Relevant information: - Historical context: Radioactive decay laws developed by Rutherford and Soddy (1902). - Example: C-14 ($$ T_{1/2} \approx 5730 \, \text{years} $$) uses similar calculations for dating. - Applications: Radiometric dating, medical isotopes. - Experimental context: Decay rates measured via Geiger counters or scintillation detectors. - Q14: Which one of the following is NOT radioactive? - Options: - (a) Astatine - (b) Francium - (c) Tritium - (d) Zirconium - Answer: - (d) Zirconium - Analysis: - Nuclear chemistry principles: - Zirconium has stable isotopes (e.g., Zr-90, ~51% abundance), used in reactors due to low neutron absorption (~0.18 barns). - Quantitative aspect: Astatine (At-211, $$ T_{1/2} \approx 7.2 \, \text{h} $$), francium (Fr-223, $$ T_{1/2} \approx 22 \, \text{min} $$), tritium ($$ ^3\text{H} $$, $$ T_{1/2} \approx 12.3 \, \text{y} $$) decay via alpha or beta emission. - Why (d) is correct: - Zirconium’s stable isotopes lack spontaneous decay. - Why not others: - (a) Astatine: All isotopes radioactive, used in targeted alpha therapy. - (b) Francium: Highly unstable, decays rapidly. - (c) Tritium: Beta emitter, used in fusion research. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Zirconium’s nuclear use identified in the 1950s. - Example: Zr-90’s stability contrasts with U-238 ($$ T_{1/2} \approx 4.5 \times 10^9 \, \text{y} $$). - Applications: Nuclear reactors, medical isotopes. - Experimental context: Radioactivity tested via gamma spectroscopy. - Q15: Match List I with List II and select the correct answer using the codes given below the lists: - List-I (Characteristic): - A. Ionic compounds are insoluble in alcohol. - 2. Ionic compounds in the solid state are good conductors of electricity. - Options: - (a) Only 1 - (b) Only 2 - (c) Both 1 and 2 - (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Answer: - (a) Only 1 - Analysis: - Chemical principles: - Statement 1: Ionic compounds (e.g., NaCl) are insoluble in low-polarity solvents like ethanol (dielectric constant ~25 vs. water’s ~80), due to high lattice energy ($$ \Delta H_{\text{lattice}} \propto \frac{q_1 q_2}{r} $$). - Statement 2: Solid ionic compounds have fixed ions, preventing conductivity; molten or aqueous states enable ion mobility ($$ \sigma \approx 10 \, \text{S/m} $$ for NaCl solution). - Why (a) is correct: - Statement 1 is true; statement 2 is false. - Why not others: - (b), (c) Statement 2 is false. - (d) Statement 1 is true. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Ionic bonding theory developed by Kossel (1916). - Example: NaCl solubility in ethanol ~0.01 g/L vs. ~360 g/L in water. - Applications: Electrolytes, industrial chemistry. - Experimental context: Conductivity tested via impedance spectroscopy. - Q17: Regarding the atom of a chemical element, the magnetic quantum number refers to: - Options: - (a) orientation - (b) shape - (c) size - (d) spin - Answer: - (a) orientation - Analysis: - Quantum mechanics principles: - Magnetic quantum number ($$ m_l $$) defines orbital orientation in a magnetic field, ranging from $$ -l $$ to $$ +l $$. Q18: Which one of the following statements is correct? - Options: - (a) Liquid sodium is employed as a coolant in nuclear reactors - (b) Calcium carbonate is an ingredient of toothpaste - (c) Bordeaux mixture consists of sodium sulphate and lime - Options: - (a) Ammonium chloride and zinc chloride - (b) Sodium chloride and calcium chloride - (c) Magnesium chloride and zinc chloride - (d) Ammonium chloride and calcium chloride - Answer: - (a) Ammonium chloride and zinc chloride - Analysis: - Electrochemical principles: - Dry cells use a paste of ammonium chloride ($$ \text{NH}_4\text{Cl} $$) and zinc chloride ($$ \text{ZnCl}_2 $$) as electrolytes, enabling ion transport: $$ \text{Zn} + 2\text{NH}_4^+ \to \text{Zn}^{2+} + 2\text{NH}_3 + \text{H}_2 $$. - Quantitative aspect: Conductivity ~10–20 S/m supports ~1.5 V output. - Why (a) is correct: - $$ \text{NH}_4\text{Cl} $$ and $$ \text{ZnCl}_2 $$ stabilize the electrolyte paste in zinc-carbon cells. - Q21: Hydrogen fuel cell vehicles produce one of the following as "exhaust": - Options: - (a) NH₃ - (b) CH₄ - (c) H₂O - (d) H₂O₂ - Answer: - (c) H₂O - Analysis: - Chemical and electrochemical principles: - Hydrogen fuel cells (e.g., proton-exchange membrane, PEM) generate electricity via the redox reaction: $$ 2\text{H}_2 + \text{O}_2 \to 2\text{H}_2\text{O} $$, with $$ \Delta H^\circ = -286 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$ per mole of water formed (liquid state). - Anode: $$ \text{H}_2 \to 2\text{H}^+ + 2e^- $$, $$ E^\circ_{\text{anode}} = 0 \, \text{V} $$. - Cathode: $$ \text{O}_2 + 4\text{H}^+ + 4e^- \to 2\text{H}_2\text{O} $$, $$ E^\circ_{\text{cathode}} \approx 1.23 \, \text{V} $$. - Overall cell potential: $$ E^\circ_{\text{cell}} \approx 1.23 \, \text{V} $$, though practical voltages are ~0.7 V due to losses (ohmic, activation, concentration). - Quantitative aspect: Efficiency ~60% (vs. ~30% for internal combustion engines), with power output ~50–100 kW for vehicles like the Toyota Mirai. - Why (c) is correct: - Water is the sole exhaust product, produced as liquid or vapor depending on operating temperature (~80°C for PEM cells), making fuel cells a clean energy technology. - Why not others: - (a) Ammonia ($$ \text{NH}_3 $$) requires nitrogen, not present in the reaction. - (b) Methane ($$ \text{CH}_4 $$) is a fuel, not a product. - (d) Hydrogen peroxide ($$ \text{H}_2\text{O}_2 $$) is unstable and not formed; its formation would require specific catalysts and conditions not present in fuel cells. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Fuel cells were invented by William Grove (1839); PEM technology advanced in the 1990s for automotive use. - Example: A fuel cell vehicle with a 5 kg H₂ tank produces ~40 kg of water over 500 km, emitting zero CO₂. - Applications: Electric vehicles (e.g., Honda Clarity), stationary power, aerospace (e.g., Apollo missions used fuel cells). - Experimental context: Fuel cell performance tested via polarization curves (voltage vs. current density) and impedance spectroscopy to assess efficiency and losses. - Broader context: Hydrogen fuel cells support decarbonization, though challenges include H₂ production (often via methane reforming, ~8 kg CO₂/kg H₂) and infrastructure costs. - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis lacks detail on reaction mechanisms, efficiency calculations, and practical challenges. The Gibbs free energy ($$ \Delta G^\circ = -237 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$) indicates spontaneity, with efficiency given by $$ \eta = \frac{\Delta G}{\Delta H} \approx 83\% $$ theoretically, reduced by irreversibilities. Catalysts (e.g., Pt) lower activation energy but increase costs. - Q22: The function of heavy water in a nuclear reactor is to: - Options: - (a) slow down the speed of neutrons - (b) increase the speed of neutrons - (c) cool down the reactor - (d) stop the nuclear reaction - Answer: - (a) slow down the speed of neutrons - Analysis: - Nuclear physics principles: - Heavy water ($$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$) acts as a neutron moderator, slowing fast neutrons (~2 MeV from U-235 fission) to thermal energies (~0.025 eV) via elastic collisions. Deuterium’s higher mass (2 u vs. 1 u for H) reduces energy loss per collision, with a moderation factor ~1200. - Thermal neutrons enhance U-235 fission cross-section ($$ \sigma_f \approx 584 \, \text{barns} $$ vs. ~1 barn for fast neutrons). - Quantitative aspect: Moderation requires ~25–30 collisions in $$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$, with scattering cross-section $$ \sigma_s \approx 7.6 \, \text{barns} $$ and low absorption ($$ \sigma_{\text{abs}} \approx 0.0005 \, \text{barns} $$). - Why (a) is correct: - Moderation enables sustained chain reactions in reactors like CANDU, using natural uranium (0.7% U-235). - Why not others: - (b) Increasing neutron speed reduces fission probability. - (c) Cooling is handled by separate systems (e.g., light water or sodium); $$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$’s specific heat (~4.2 J/g·°C) is secondary. - (d) Control rods (e.g., boron, $$ \sigma_{\text{abs}} \approx 760 \, \text{barns} $$) stop reactions by absorbing neutrons. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Heavy water reactors developed in the 1940s (e.g., Canada’s NRX); key to early nuclear research. - Example: CANDU reactors use ~50 tons of $$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$, enabling 700 MW power output with unenriched fuel. - Applications: Nuclear power, neutron scattering experiments. - Experimental context: Neutron energy spectra measured via time-of-flight techniques or activation analysis. - Broader context: $$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$’s low absorption allows efficient use of natural uranium, but production is energy-intensive (~10 GJ/kg). - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis omits the physics of moderation and reactor design. The slowing-down power ($$ \xi \Sigma_s $$) of $$ \text{D}_2\text{O} $$ is higher than graphite but lower than $$ \text{H}_2\text{O} $$, balancing moderation and absorption. Safety concerns include tritium production ($$ \text{D} + n \to ^3\text{H} $$). - Q23: Water can dissolve more substances than any other liquid because: - Options: - (a) It is dipolar in nature - (b) It is a good conductor of heat - (c) It has high value of specific heat - (d) It is an oxide of hydrogen - Answer: - (a) It is dipolar in nature - Analysis: - Chemical and physical principles: - Water’s dipole moment ($$ \mu \approx 1.85 \, \text{D} $$) arises from its bent geometry (104.5°) and electronegative oxygen ($$ \chi = 3.44 $$). Its high dielectric constant ($$ \epsilon_r \approx 80 $$) weakens ionic interactions, enabling dissolution of salts (e.g., NaCl, $$ K_{sp} \approx 36 \, \text{g/100 mL} $$). - Hydrogen bonding (strength ~20 kJ/mol) solvates polar molecules (e.g., glucose) and ions via ion-dipole interactions. - Quantitative aspect: Solvation free energy ($$ \Delta G_{\text{solv}} = \Delta H - T\Delta S $$) favors dissolution when lattice energy is overcome (e.g., NaCl: $$ \Delta H_{\text{lattice}} \approx 787 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$). - Why (a) is correct: - Dipolarity drives water’s universal solvent properties for ionic and polar substances. - Why not others: - (b) Thermal conductivity (~0.6 W/m·K) is unrelated to solubility. - (c) Specific heat ($$ c \approx 4.18 \, \text{J/g·°C} $$) affects heat retention, not dissolution. - (d) “Oxide of hydrogen” describes composition, not solvent ability. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Water’s solvent properties studied since alchemy; quantified in the 19th century via solution chemistry. - Example: Water dissolves ~360 g/L NaCl, ~100 g/L sucrose, but <0.01 g/L non-polar benzene. - Applications: Chemical synthesis, biological systems (e.g., cytosol), industrial processes. - Experimental context: Solubility measured via titration or conductivity; dielectric constant via capacitance. - Broader context: Water’s polarity enables life processes (e.g., protein folding) but limits non-polar solubility, requiring surfactants in industries. - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis lacks detail on solvation mechanisms and quantitative data. Ionisation potential gradually decreases along a period - 2. In a group of elements, electron affinity decreases as the atomic weight increases - 3. In a given period, electronegativity decreases as the atomic number increases - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 only - (c) 1 and 3 - (d) 2 and 3 - Answer: - (b) 2 only - Analysis: - Periodic trends and quantum mechanics: - Statement 1 (False): Ionisation energy (IE, energy to remove the outermost electron) increases across a period due to increasing effective nuclear charge ($$ Z_{\text{eff}} = Z - \sigma $$, where $$ Z $$ is the atomic number and $$ \sigma $$ is the shielding constant). Electrons are added to the same shell, minimally increasing shielding, while $$ Z $$ rises, reducing atomic radius ($$ r $$) and increasing IE. Mathematically: $$ IE \propto \frac{Z_{\text{eff}}}{r} $$. Example: Period 2, Li ($$ IE = 520 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$, $$ r = 152 \, \text{pm} $$) to Ne ($$ IE = 2081 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$, $$ r = 70 \, \text{pm} $$). Quantum mechanically, higher $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$ raises orbital energy, tightening electron binding. - Statement 2 (True): Electron affinity (EA, energy released when an electron is added) generally decreases down a group as atomic weight and radius increase. Additional electron shells increase $$ r $$ and $$ \sigma $$, reducing $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$ for the incoming electron. Example: Group 17, F ($$ EA = -328 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$, $$ r = 72 \, \text{pm} $$), Cl ($$ EA = -349 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), Br ($$ EA = -325 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), I ($$ EA = -295 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$). Anomaly: Cl > F due to F’s compact 2p orbitals causing electron-electron repulsion (Coulomb energy $$ \propto \frac{1}{r_{12}} $$), reducing EA. Down the group, larger orbitals mitigate this effect. - Statement 3 (False): Electronegativity (EN, tendency to attract bonding electrons) increases across a period due to higher $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$ and smaller $$ r $$, enhancing nuclear pull on shared electrons. Pauling scale: Li ($$ EN = 0.98 $$) to F ($$ EN = 3.98 $$) in period 2. Mathematically: $$ EN \propto Z_{\text{eff}} $$. Across a period, $$ \sigma $$ changes little, so EN rises with $$ Z $$. - Why (b) is correct: - Only statement 2 aligns with periodic trends, accounting for the general decrease in EA down a group, despite anomalies like Cl > F. - Why not others: - (a) Statement 1 is false (IE increases across a period). - (c) Both 1 and 3 are false (EN increases across a period). - (d) Statement 3 is false, negating the combination. - Relevant information: - Historical context: Periodic trends systematized by Mendeleev (1869); Pauling’s EN scale (1932) quantified bonding tendencies. EA measurements refined in the 20th century via photoelectron spectroscopy. - Example: Group 1 reactivity (low IE, e.g., Cs: $$ IE = 376 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$) drives explosive reactions with water; high EN of F explains HF’s strong bond ($$ D_0 \approx 570 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$). - Applications: IE influences ionization in mass spectrometry; EA and EN guide catalyst design (e.g., F’s reactivity in fluorination). - Experimental context: IE measured via photoionization; EA via electron capture detectors; EN inferred from bond energies or NMR shifts. - Broader context: Trends underpin chemical reactivity (e.g., alkali metals’ low IE vs. halogens’ high EA) and material properties (e.g., semiconductors). Anomalies (e.g., Cl > F) highlight quantum effects like orbital overlap. - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis lacks quantum mechanical insights and anomalies. The Slater shielding model approximates $$ \sigma $$, explaining why $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$ rises across periods but falls down groups. The Cl > F EA anomaly arises from F’s higher electron density ($$ \rho \propto \frac{1}{r^3} $$), increasing repulsion. These trends predict reactivity (e.g., F’s aggressive bonding in PTFE synthesis). - Q42: Match List-I (Oxidation number) with List II (The element) and select the correct answer using the codes given below the lists: - List-I: - A. Gypsum’s role in cement involves surface adsorption, slowing C₃A’s rapid hydration ($$ k \approx 10^3 \, \text{L/mol·s} $$). - Q45: Assertion (A): In the periodic table of chemical elements, electron affinity is always found to increase from top to bottom in a group. - Reason (R): In a group, the atomic radii generally increase from top to bottom. - Options: - (a) Both A and R are individually true and R is the correct explanation of A - (b) Both A and R are individually true but R is not the correct explanation of A - (c) A is true but R is false - (d) A is false but R is true - Answer: - (d) A is false but R is true - Analysis: - Periodic trends: - Assertion: False. Electron affinity (EA) generally decreases down a group due to increasing atomic radius ($$ r $$) and shielding ($$ \sigma $$), reducing $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$. Example: Group 17, F ($$ EA = -328 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), Cl ($$ EA = -349 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), Br ($$ EA = -325 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), I ($$ EA = -295 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$). The Cl > F anomaly arises from F’s compact orbitals (electron repulsion energy $$ \propto \frac{1}{r} $$). Larger atoms (e.g., I) have lower EA due to weaker nuclear attraction: $$ EA \propto \frac{Z_{\text{eff}}}{r} $$. - Reason: True. Atomic radii increase down a group due to additional electron shells (e.g., Li: 152 pm, Cs: 265 pm), increasing $$ \sigma $$ and reducing $$ Z_{\text{eff}} $$. - Explanation: R does not support A; larger radii decrease EA by reducing nuclear pull, contradicting A’s claim. - Why (d) is correct: - A is false (EA decreases); R is true (radii increase) but does not explain A. - Why not others: - (a, b) A is false. - (c) R is true. - Relevant information: - Historical context: EA trends quantified in the 20th century via electron affinity measurements (e.g., laser photodetachment). - Example: F’s high EA drives HF’s acidity ($$ K_a \approx 10^{-3} $$); I’s low EA reduces HI’s reactivity. - Applications: EA influences halogenation reactions, semiconductor doping. - Experimental context: EA measured via anion photoelectron spectroscopy; radii via X-ray crystallography. - Broader context: EA trends predict halogen reactivity and bond strengths (e.g., F₂’s low dissociation energy: 159 kJ/mol). - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis omits the Cl > F anomaly and quantum effects. The Hartree-Fock model explains F’s lower EA due to higher electron repulsion in 2p orbitals. Radii increase follows quantum shell additions ($$ n^2 $$-dependence of orbital size). - Q46: Assertion (A): Coal-based thermal power stations contribute to acid rain. - Reason (R): Oxides of carbon are emitted when coal burns. - Options: - (a) Both A and R are individually true and R is the correct explanation of A - (b) Both A and R are individually true but R is not the correct explanation of A - (c) A is true but R is false - (d) A is false but R is true - Answer: - (b) Both A and R are individually true but R is not the correct explanation of A - Analysis: - Environmental chemistry: - Assertion: True. Acid rain (pH < 5.6) damages ecosystems. - Reason: True. Coal burning produces CO ($$ \text{C} + \frac{1}{2}\text{O}_2 \to \text{CO} $$) and CO₂ ($$ \text{C} + \text{O}_2 \to \text{CO}_2 $$), with CO₂ dominating (~2.5 tons/ton coal). - Explanation: R does not explain A. SO₂ oxidation involves radical catalysis (OH·): $$ \text{SO}_2 + \text{OH}· \to \text{HSO}_3· $$, enhancing H₂SO₄ formation. CO₂’s weak acidity ($$ \text{pH} \approx 5.6 $$) is negligible in acid rain contexts. - Q47: Consider the following statements: - Statements: - 1. Baking soda is used in fire extinguishers - 2. UHMWPE competes but has lower thermal stability (~150°C vs. Kevlar’s ~400°C). - Q61: The offending substance in the liquor tragedies leading to blindness etc. is: - Options: - (a) ethyl alcohol - (b) amyl alcohol - (c) benzyl alcohol - (d) methyl alcohol - Answer: - (d) methyl alcohol - Analysis: - Toxicology and metabolic pathways: - Methyl alcohol (CH₃OH, methanol) is highly toxic when ingested, metabolized by alcohol dehydrogenase (ADH) and aldehyde dehydrogenase (ALDH): - $$ \text{CH}_3\text{OH} \xrightarrow{\text{ADH}} \text{HCHO} \xrightarrow{\text{ALDH}} \text{HCOOH} $$. - Formaldehyde (HCHO) damages proteins; formic acid (HCOOH, $$ K_a \approx 1.8 \times 10^{-4} $$) causes metabolic acidosis (pH < 7.35) and inhibits cytochrome c oxidase, leading to optic nerve damage and blindness (LD₅₀ ~1 g/kg body weight). - Quantitative aspect: Blood methanol levels >20 mg/dL cause toxicity; formate levels >2 mM correlate with blindness. - (a) Ethyl alcohol (C₂H₅OH): Safe in moderation (metabolizes to acetaldehyde, then acetate); used therapeutically to compete with methanol for ADH ($$ K_m^{\text{methanol}} \approx 10 \, \text{mM}, K_m^{\text{ethanol}} \approx 1 \, \text{mM} $$). - (b) Amyl alcohol (C₅H₁₁OH): Less toxic, used in solvents; mild CNS depressant. - (c) Benzyl alcohol (C₆H₅CH₂OH): Low toxicity, used as a preservative; metabolizes to benzoic acid. - Why (d) is correct: - Methanol’s metabolic products directly cause severe symptoms, including optic neuropathy. - Why not others: - Other alcohols lack methanol’s toxic metabolites or potency. - Enhanced analysis: - The original analysis lacks metabolic and therapeutic details. Experimental methods: Blood formate via HPLC; retinal damage via electroretinography. Applications: Public health campaigns target illicit liquor (e.g., India, 2019 hooch tragedies: ~150 deaths). In the production of plastic foams - 2. In the production of tubeless tyres - 3. In cleaning certain electronic components - 4. As pressurizing agents in aerosol cans - Options: - (a) 1, 2 and 3 only - (b) 4 only - (c) 1, 3 and 4 only - (d) 1, 2, 3 and 4 - Answer: - (c) 1, 3 and 4 only - Analysis: - Chemical Context: - Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs, e.g., CFCl₃, CF₂Cl₂) are stable halogenated hydrocarbons with C-F and C-Cl bonds (bond energies ~485 kJ/mol and ~327 kJ/mol, respectively). BOD (~20 mg/L in polluted rivers) and COD (~50 mg/L) quantify organic load. Historical context: DO monitoring standardized in the 1970s. Experimental methods: DO via electrochemical sensors; BOD via incubation. Applications: River restoration, fisheries. Global perspective: ~50% of rivers globally have DO <6 mg/L. Limitations: The original ignores seasonal DO variations (e.g., lower in summer, ~10% drop). - Q91: Which one of the following is produced during the formation of photochemical smog? - Options: - (a) Hydrocarbons - (b) Nitrogen Oxide - (c) Ozone - (d) Methane - Answer: - (c) Ozone - Analysis: - Photochemical Smog Chemistry: - Smog forms from NO₂ photolysis: - $$ \text{NO}_2 + h\nu \rightarrow \text{NO} + \text{O} $$, $$ \lambda < 400 \, \text{nm} $$. - $$ \text{O} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{O}_3 $$, [O₃] ~0.1–0.5 ppm in smog. - VOCs and NOₓ are precursors, not products. - (a, d) Hydrocarbons, methane: Reactants. - (b) NOₓ: Consumed, not produced. - Why Not Others?: - Ozone is the primary smog product. - Enhanced Analysis: - The original omits secondary products (e.g., PAN: $$ \text{RCOOONO}_2 $$). O₃ formation rate ~10 ppb/h in urban areas. Historical context: Smog studied in LA (1950s). Experimental methods: O₃ via UV photometry; VOCs via GC-MS. Applications: Air quality regulations. Global perspective: Smog impacts ~25% of urban populations (e.g., Delhi, AQI >200). Limitations: The original ignores O₃’s health effects (e.g., respiratory damage at >0.07 ppm). - Q92: Which of the following substances are found in the beach sands of many parts of Kerala? - Statements: - 1. Ilmenite - 2. Zircon - 3. Sillimanite - 4. Tungsten (e.g., wolframite): Rare in beach sands; found in granite deposits. - Why Not Others?: - (a, c) Include false statement 4. - (d) Excludes true statement 3. - Enhanced Analysis: - The original omits mineral properties. Ilmenite’s density (~4.7 g/cm³) aids separation. Historical context: Chavara mining since the 1920s. Experimental methods: Mineral identification via XRD; Ti content via ICP-MS. Applications: TiO₂ pigments (~5 Mt/year globally). Global perspective: Kerala supplies ~10% of global ilmenite. Limitations: The original ignores environmental impacts (e.g., coastal erosion from mining). - Q93: Which of the following causes/cause acid rain? - Statements: - 1. Oxides of Hydrogen - 2. Oxides of Nitrogen - 3. Oxides of Sulphur - Options: - (a) 1 and 2 only - (b) 3 only - (c) 2 and 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (c) 2 and 3 only - Analysis: - Acid Rain Chemistry: - 2. NOₓ: Forms HNO₃: - $$ 2\text{NO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{HNO}_3 + \text{HNO}_2 $$, $$ \text{pK}_a^{\text{HNO}_3} \approx -1.3 $$. - 3. Oxides of Hydrogen: H₂O is neutral; H₂O₂ is minor, not a primary cause. - Why Not Others?: - (a, d) Include false statement 1. - (b) Excludes true statement 2. - Enhanced Analysis: - The original omits emission sources.
