Limitations: The original ignores dry deposition (e.g., SO₂ gas effects). - Q94: Excessive release of the pollutant carbon monoxide (CO) into the air may produce a condition in which oxygen supply in the human body decreases. What causes this condition? - Options: - (a) When inhaled into the human body CO is converted into CO₂ - (b) The inhaled CO has much higher affinity for haemoglobin as compared to oxygen - (c) The inhaled CO destroys the chemical structure of haemoglobin - (d) The inhaled CO adversely affects the respiratory centre in the brain - Answer: - (b) The inhaled CO has much higher affinity for haemoglobin as compared to oxygen - Analysis: - Biochemical Context: - CO binds haemoglobin ~200 times stronger than O₂: - $$ \text{Hb} + \text{CO} \rightarrow \text{HbCO} $$, $$ K_d^{\text{CO}} \approx 10^{-8} \, \text{M} $$, $$ K_d^{\text{O}_2} \approx 10^{-6} \, \text{M} $$. - HbCO reduces O₂ transport, causing hypoxia (LC₅₀ ~1000 ppm). - (a) CO to CO₂: Does not occur biologically. - (c) CO does not destroy haemoglobin. - (d) Brain effects are secondary to hypoxia. - Why Not Others?: - Only (b) explains the mechanism. - Enhanced Analysis: - The original omits binding details. CO’s triple bond ($$ \text{C≡O} $$, ~1070 kJ/mol) enhances Hb affinity via π-backbonding. Historical context: CO poisoning noted in the 19th century. Experimental methods: HbCO via CO-oximetry; exposure via CO monitors. Applications: CO detectors, hyperbaric O₂ therapy. Global perspective: CO causes ~500,000 deaths/year. Limitations: The original ignores chronic exposure effects (e.g., neurological damage at ~50 ppm). - Q95: Consider the following: - Statements: - 1. Carbon dioxide - 2. Oxides of nitrogen - 3. Oxides of sulphur - Which of the above is/are the emission/emissions from coal combustion at thermal power plants? - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 and 3 only - (c) 1 and 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Coal, a heterogeneous mixture of carbon (~60–80%), sulfur (~0.5–5%), nitrogen (~1–2%), and trace elements (e.g., Hg, As), undergoes combustion in oxygen-rich environments at ~1200–1500°C. Combustion is an exothermic redox process, converting chemical potential energy into heat and gaseous byproducts. The stoichiometry and thermodynamics of these reactions govern emission profiles. - Statement 1 (CO₂): Carbon in coal (sp²-hybridized in polycyclic aromatic structures) reacts with O₂: - $$ \text{C} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{CO}_2 $$, $$ \Delta H = -393.5 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$, $$ \Delta G = -394.4 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$. - The reaction is thermodynamically favored due to the strong C=O bonds (~803 kJ/mol). For anthracite (~80% C), ~2.2 kg CO₂/kg coal is produced. CO₂, a linear molecule with zero dipole moment, absorbs IR radiation (~15 μm), contributing to greenhouse warming (radiative forcing ~1.7 W/m²). - Statement 2 (NOₓ): Nitrogen oxides form via two mechanisms: - Thermal NOₓ: Atmospheric N₂ reacts at high temperatures via the Zeldovich mechanism: - $$ \text{N}_2 + \text{O} \rightarrow \text{NO} + \text{N} $$, $$ E_a \approx 315 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$. - $$ \text{N} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{NO} + \text{O} $$. - Rate: $$ k \approx 1.8 \times 10^8 \, \text{e}^{-38000/T} \, \text{L/mol·s} $$, significant above 1300°C. - Fuel NOₓ: Coal’s organic nitrogen (e.g., pyrrolic, pyridinic groups) oxidizes: - $$ \text{R-N} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{NO} + \text{products} $$. - NO oxidizes to NO₂: $$ 2\text{NO} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow 2\text{NO}_2 $$. Emissions: ~5–10 g NOₓ/kg coal; ~1–2 g/kWh. NO₂, with a bent structure, contributes to acid rain (HNO₃ formation). - Statement 3 (SOₓ): Sulfur in coal (e.g., pyrite, FeS₂) oxidizes: - $$ \text{S} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{SO}_2 $$, $$ \Delta H = -296.8 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$. - Partial oxidation to SO₃: $$ 2\text{SO}_2 + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow 2\text{SO}_3 $$, catalyzed by ash or V₂O₅ in flue gas. - SO₂/SO₃ form acids in water: $$ \text{SO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_3 $$, $$ \text{SO}_3 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_4 $$. Emissions: ~10–20 g SO₂/kg coal (1% S). SO₂’s polar structure enhances solubility, driving acid rain (pH <5.6). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Excludes NOₓ and SOₓ, which are inevitable due to coal’s N and S content and high-temperature combustion. - (b) Incorrect: Omits CO₂, the dominant emission (~90% of carbon mass), violating mass balance of carbon oxidation. - (c) Incorrect: Excludes NOₓ, which forms via thermal and fuel mechanisms, detectable in all coal plants (~1 g/kWh). - (d) Correct: All three are fundamental products of coal combustion, driven by elemental composition and reaction conditions. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Combustion is entropy-driven ($$ \Delta S > 0 $$) due to gas formation; equilibrium constants favor products (e.g., $$ K_p^{\text{CO}_2} \approx 10^{40} $$ at 1500 K). NOₓ formation is kinetically limited, explaining lower yields. - Quantum Considerations: C=O, N=O, and S=O bonds involve π-overlaps, stabilizing products. NO₂’s unpaired electron enhances reactivity. - Historical Context: Coal plants, operational since the 19th century, became major polluters post-1950s industrialization. The 1970 Clean Air Act (USA) and India’s 1981 Air Act targeted NOₓ/SOₓ emissions. - Experimental Methods: CO₂ via non-dispersive IR spectroscopy; NOₓ via chemiluminescence (NO + O₃ → NO₂* + hν); SOₓ via UV fluorescence. Trace metals (Hg, As) via ICP-MS. - Applications: Mitigation includes flue gas desulfurization (FGD, ~90% SO₂ removal via CaCO₃) and selective catalytic reduction (SCR, ~80% NOₓ reduction via NH₃). Carbon capture (CCS) traps ~90% CO₂ but is costly (~$50/ton). - Global Perspective: Coal supplies ~27% of global energy (2025); China (~50%) and India (~10%) dominate CO₂ emissions (~15 Gt/year). SO₂ emissions: ~20 Mt/year (China), ~10 Mt/year (India). - Limitations: The original omits trace pollutants (e.g., Hg ~0.01–0.1 mg/kg coal, bioaccumulative), CO from incomplete combustion (~0.1 g/kWh), and CCS scalability issues (<1% global adoption). It also ignores regional coal variability (e.g., lignite’s higher S content). - Q96: What is the role of ultraviolet (UV) radiation in the water purification systems? - Statements: - 1. It inactivates/kills the harmful microorganisms in water - 2. It removes all the undesirable odours from the water - 3. It quickens the sedimentation of solid particles, removes turbidity and improves the clarity of water - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 and 3 only - (c) 1 and 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (a) 1 only - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - UV-C radiation (200–280 nm, photon energy ~4.4–6.2 eV) induces photochemical reactions in water purification by exciting molecular orbitals, primarily targeting microbial DNA. - Statement 1: UV-C photons are absorbed by DNA’s purine/pyrimidine bases (absorption peak ~260 nm, ε ≈ 10⁴ M⁻¹cm⁻¹), forming cyclobutane pyrimidine dimers (e.g., thymine-thymine): - $$ \text{DNA} + h\nu \rightarrow \text{DNA-dimer} $$. - This disrupts replication/transcription, inactivating microbes (e.g., E. coli, 99.9% kill at ~6 mJ/cm²; Cryptosporidium, ~10 mJ/cm²). The process is quantum-driven, with photon energy exceeding DNA bond energies (~4 eV). - Statement 2: Odours arise from volatile organic compounds (VOCs, e.g., geosmin, C₁₂H₂₂O) or H₂S. UV-C lacks sufficient energy to break C-C (~347 kJ/mol) or C-H (~413 kJ/mol) bonds in VOCs. Activated carbon (adsorption, surface area ~1000 m²/g) or ozonation (O₃, E° = 2.07 V) is required for odour removal. - Statement 3: Turbidity (colloidal particles, ~0.1–10 μm) scatters light (Rayleigh/Mie scattering) and is removed via coagulation (e.g., Al₂(SO₄)₃ reduces ζ-potential) or filtration. UV-C does not alter particle charge or sedimentation kinetics (Stokes’ law: $$ v \propto r^2 $$). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Correct: UV-C’s role is limited to disinfection, driven by photochemical damage to nucleic acids. - (b) Incorrect: Neither odours (chemical issue) nor turbidity (physical issue) are addressed by UV’s photochemical mechanism. - (c) Incorrect: Statement 3 is false, as UV does not influence sedimentation or turbidity. - (d) Incorrect: Includes false statements 2 and 3. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: UV disinfection is non-equilibrium, driven by photon absorption; energy transfer efficiency ~10–20% in UV lamps (Hg-vapor, ~254 nm). - Quantum Considerations: UV-C excites π→π* transitions in DNA, with quantum yield ~0.1 for dimer formation. No equivalent mechanism exists for VOCs or particles. - Historical Context: UV disinfection emerged in the 1990s as a chemical-free alternative to chlorination, spurred by Cryptosporidium outbreaks (e.g., Milwaukee, 1993). - Experimental Methods: Microbial inactivation via colony-forming unit (CFU) assays; UV dose via radiometry ($$ D = I \cdot t $$, ~10–40 mJ/cm²). VOCs via GC-MS; turbidity via nephelometry. - Applications: UV treats ~10% of global municipal water, effective for wastewater reuse and point-of-use systems. - Global Perspective: Europe/USA lead UV adoption (~20% of plants); India’s UV systems growing (e.g., Delhi’s 100 MLD plants). Energy use: ~0.01 kWh/m³. - Limitations: The original omits UV’s ineffectiveness against chemical pollutants (e.g., pesticides, ~1 ppb persistence) and dependence on water clarity (turbidity >5 NTU reduces efficacy). It also ignores lamp maintenance (Hg leakage risks) and energy costs. - Q97: A team of scientists at Brookhaven National Laboratory including those from India created the heaviest anti-matter (anti-helium nucleus). What is/are the implication/implications of the creation of anti-matter? - Statements: - 1. It will make mineral prospecting and oil exploration easier and cheaper - 2. It will help probe the possibility of the existence of stars and galaxies made of anti-matter - 3. It will help understand the evolution of the universe - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 and 3 only - (c) 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (b) 2 and 3 only - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Anti-matter (e.g., anti-helium, ⁴He̅) consists of antiparticles (antiprotons, antineutrons) with opposite charge but identical mass to matter. At Brookhaven’s RHIC, anti-helium forms via high-energy Au-Au collisions (~200 GeV/nucleon), with low yield (~10⁻¹² per collision). Annihilation with matter releases energy: - $$ \text{p}^+ + \text{p}^- \rightarrow 2\gamma $$, $$ E = 2m_p c^2 \approx 1.8 \, \text{GeV} $$. - Statement 1: Mineral prospecting uses techniques like seismic surveys or gamma-ray spectroscopy (e.g., ⁴⁰K decay). Anti-matter’s production cost (~$10¹¹/g) and short lifetime (annihilation in ~ns) make it impractical compared to isotopes like ¹⁸F in PET (cost ~$100/MBq). No quantum or thermodynamic basis supports anti-matter’s use here. - Statement 2: Anti-matter detection in cosmic rays (e.g., via AMS-02 on ISS, sensitive to ~1 anti-helium event/year) could indicate anti-matter stars/galaxies. Quantum chromodynamics predicts anti-nuclei formation in high-energy environments (e.g., early universe, neutron stars), testing grand unified theories. - Statement 3: Anti-matter studies probe matter-antimatter asymmetry ($$ n_b/n_\gamma \approx 10^{-10} $$). CP violation (e.g., kaon decay, $$ \epsilon \approx 2 \times 10^{-3} $$) and baryogenesis models explain why matter dominates post-Big Bang, shaping universe evolution. - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Anti-matter’s energy-intensive production violates economic and practical feasibility for prospecting. - (b) Correct: Statements 2 and 3 align with anti-matter’s role in fundamental physics, probing cosmic and evolutionary questions. - (c) Incorrect: Excludes statement 2, which is valid given anti-matter’s cosmic detection potential. - (d) Incorrect: Includes false statement 1. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Anti-matter production is entropically unfavorable, requiring ~10¹⁵ eV/g. Annihilation is exothermic, with complete mass-energy conversion. - Quantum Considerations: Anti-helium’s formation involves quark-gluon plasma (QGP, ~10¹² K), governed by QCD. Detection relies on distinct charge-to-mass ratios (e.g., $$ Z/A = -2/4 $$). - Historical Context: Dirac’s theory (1928) predicted anti-matter; anti-proton discovered 1955; anti-helium at RHIC (2011). India’s STAR experiment contributed to detection. - Experimental Methods: Anti-nuclei via time-of-flight spectroscopy; annihilation via calorimetry (E > 1 GeV). Cosmic ray analysis via magnetic spectrometers. - Applications: Limited to fundamental research; no industrial use due to cost and instability. - Global Perspective: CERN, RHIC lead anti-matter research; AMS-02 data (~2025) constrains anti-matter galaxies to <10⁻⁶ of visible matter. - Limitations: The original omits anti-matter’s low yield, detection challenges (background noise ~10⁴:1), and theoretical implications (e.g., Sakharov conditions for baryogenesis). - Q98: Acid rain is caused by the pollution of environment by: - Options: - (a) Carbon dioxide and nitrogen - (b) Carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide - (c) Ozone and carbon dioxide - (d) Nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide - Answer: - (d) Nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Acid rain (pH <5.6) results from strong acids (HNO₃, H₂SO₄) formed by gas-phase reactions of atmospheric pollutants with water. Acid strength depends on dissociation constants ($$ \text{pK}_a $$). - Option (a): CO₂ forms H₂CO₃: - $$ \text{CO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \leftrightarrow \text{H}_2\text{CO}_3 $$, $$ \text{pK}_a \approx 6.4 $$. - H₂CO₃ is a weak acid, contributing minimally to rain pH (~5.6). N₂ is inert, not forming acidic oxides under ambient conditions. - Option (b): CO is non-acidic, forming no stable acid in water. CO₂’s role is negligible, as above. - Option (c): O₃, a strong oxidant (E° = 2.07 V), does not form acids; it oxidizes NO to NO₂ or SO₂ to SO₃, indirectly aiding acid rain but not as a primary pollutant. CO₂ is weak, as above. - Option (d): SO₂ forms H₂SO₃ and H₂SO₄: - $$ \text{SO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_3 $$, $$ \text{pK}_a \approx 1.9 $$. - $$ \text{SO}_3 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_4 $$, $$ \text{pK}_a \approx -3 $$. - NO₂ (from N₂O oxidation, e.g., $$ \text{N}_2\text{O} + \text{O}_3 \rightarrow 2\text{NO} $$) forms HNO₃: - $$ 2\text{NO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{HNO}_3 + \text{HNO}_2 $$, $$ \text{pK}_a^{\text{HNO}_3} \approx -1.3 $$. - These strong acids significantly lower rain pH, matching acid rain’s definition. - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: CO₂’s weak acidity and N₂’s inertness do not drive acid rain. - (b) Incorrect: CO and CO₂ lack strong acid-forming capacity. - (c) Incorrect: O₃ is not a direct acid precursor; CO₂ is insufficient. - (d) Correct: SO₂ and NO₂ (via N₂O) produce strong acids, aligning with acid rain chemistry. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: SO₃ formation is exothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx -99 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), favoring H₂SO₄ production. NO₂’s aqueous reactions are kinetically fast ($$ k \approx 10^5 \, \text{M}^{-1}\text{s}^{-1} $$). - Quantum Considerations: SO₂’s bent structure (C₂v symmetry) enhances solubility; NO₂’s unpaired electron increases reactivity. - Historical Context: Acid rain emerged as a concern in the 1970s (Scandinavia, USA); reduced by ~50% in Europe via SO₂/NOₓ controls (1990s). - Experimental Methods: Rain pH via glass electrodes; SO₂/NO₂ via FTIR or chemiluminescence. Deposition via ion chromatography. - Applications: Emission controls (FGD, SCR reduce ~90% SOₓ/NOₓ); liming neutralizes soil acidity. - Global Perspective: China (~20 Mt SO₂/year), India (~10 Mt) lead emissions; ~10% of global forests affected by acid rain. - Limitations: The original misidentifies N₂O as a major precursor (NO₂ dominates, ~70% of NOₓ). It omits dry deposition (~30% of acid input) and soil buffering (e.g., CaCO₃, $$ K_{sp} \approx 10^{-8} $$). - Q99: With reference to 'fly ash' produced by the power plants using coal as fuel, which of the following statements is/are correct? - Statements: - 1. Fly ash can be used in the production of bricks for building construction - 2. Fly ash can be used as a replacement for some of the Portland cement concrete - 3. Fly ash is made up of silicon dioxide and calcium oxide only, and does not contain any toxic elements - Options: - (a) 1 and 2 - (b) 2 only - (c) 1 and 3 - (d) 3 only - Answer: - (a) 1 and 2 - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Fly ash, a fine particulate from coal combustion, comprises amorphous aluminosilicates (~60–70% SiO₂, 10–20% Al₂O₃, 5–15% CaO) and trace metals (As, Pb, Hg). Its pozzolanic properties (reactivity with Ca(OH)₂) stem from SiO₂’s amorphous structure. - Statement 1: Fly ash bricks (~40–60% fly ash, 30% clay) leverage SiO₂’s binding properties, achieving compressive strength (~10 MPa) via: - $$ \text{SiO}_2 + \text{Ca(OH)}_2 \rightarrow \text{C-S-H} $$. - Reduces clay demand (~50%) and firing energy (~20%). - Statement 2: Fly ash (Class F: low CaO; Class C: high CaO) replaces 15–30% Portland cement, forming calcium silicate hydrate (C-S-H): - $$ \text{SiO}_2 + \text{Ca(OH)}_2 \rightarrow \text{C-S-H} $$, enhancing concrete strength (~50 MPa) and durability (reduced permeability, ~10⁻¹² m/s). - Statement 3: Fly ash contains toxic metals (As ~10 ppm, Pb ~50 ppm, Hg ~0.1 ppm), which can leach under acidic conditions (e.g., pH <4, TCLP limits: As 5 mg/L, Pb 5 mg/L). SiO₂ and CaO are major but not exclusive components. - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Correct: Statements 1 and 2 reflect fly ash’s pozzolanic utility, driven by SiO₂ reactivity. - (b) Incorrect: Excludes statement 1, which is valid due to fly ash’s role in bricks. - (c) Incorrect: Statement 3 is false, as toxic metals are present, violating compositional claims. - (d) Incorrect: Statement 3 is false; excludes true statements 1 and 2. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: C-S-H formation is exothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx -50 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), stabilizing concrete. Fly ash’s amorphous SiO₂ has higher Gibbs free energy than crystalline forms, enhancing reactivity. - Quantum Considerations: SiO₂’s disordered structure lacks long-range order, increasing surface reactivity. Trace metals form stable oxides (e.g., As₂O₃). - Historical Context: Fly ash utilization grew post-1980s (EPA regulations); India’s 1998 Fly Ash Mission promoted use. - Experimental Methods: Composition via XRF; leaching via TCLP; concrete strength via ASTM C39. Particle size (~10 μm) via laser diffraction. - Applications: Bricks (~20% of India’s fly ash, ~40 Mt/year); concrete (~15%). Also used in road embankments, mine reclamation. - Global Perspective: ~800 Mt/year fly ash globally; India utilizes ~60%, China ~70%. Regulations (e.g., India’s 2016 fly ash rules) mandate 100% use. - Limitations: The original omits trace metal risks (e.g., groundwater contamination, ~1% leaching in acidic soils), variability (Class F vs. C), and disposal challenges (~40% of India’s fly ash landfilled). - Q100: Brominated flame retardants are used in many household products like mattresses and upholstery. Why is there some concern about their use? - Statements: - 1. They are highly resistant to degradation in the environment - 2. They are able to accumulate in humans and animals - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 only - (c) Both 1 and 2 - (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Answer: - (c) Both 1 and 2 - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Brominated flame retardants (BFRs, e.g., PBDEs: C₁₂H₁₀₋ₓBrₓO) inhibit combustion by releasing Br radicals, quenching free-radical chain reactions: - $$ \text{R-Br} \xrightarrow{\Delta} \text{R} \cdot + \text{Br} \cdot $$, $$ \text{Br} \cdot + \text{R} \cdot \rightarrow \text{RBr} $$. - Statement 1: C-Br bonds (~290 kJ/mol) are stable, resisting photolysis, hydrolysis, and biodegradation (half-life ~10–100 years in soil). PBDEs’ aromatic structure (sp²-hybridized) enhances persistence. - Statement 2: PBDEs’ lipophilicity (log P ~5–7) drives bioaccumulation in fatty tissues (BCF ~10⁴–10⁵). They biomagnify through food chains, reaching ~10 ng/g lipid in human blood, disrupting thyroid hormones via aryl hydrocarbon receptor (AhR) binding ($$ K_d \approx 10^{-8} \, \text{M} $$). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Excludes statement 2, which is valid due to PBDEs’ bioaccumulation, a key toxicological concern. - (b) Incorrect: Excludes statement 1, which is true given BFRs’ environmental stability. - (c) Correct: Both statements reflect BFRs’ chemical stability and biological impacts, rooted in molecular structure and pharmacokinetics. - (d) Incorrect: Both statements are true. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: C-Br bond cleavage requires high energy (~290 kJ/mol), explaining persistence. Bioaccumulation is entropically favored in lipid phases. - Quantum Considerations: PBDEs’ conjugated π-systems stabilize molecules; Br’s electronegativity (2.96) enhances AhR binding. - Historical Context: BFRs widely used since the 1970s; restricted under Stockholm Convention (2009) due to toxicity concerns. - Experimental Methods: PBDE detection via GC-MS (LOD ~1 pg/g); toxicity via bioassays (e.g., TCDD-equivalent factors). Tissue levels via lipid extraction. - Applications: Fire safety in textiles, electronics (~0.1% BFR by weight). Alternatives: organophosphates, inorganic retardants. - Global Perspective: ~70 kt/year PBDE production (pre-ban); EU, USA banned penta-/octa-BDEs; India phasing out. ~50% of e-waste contains BFRs. - Limitations: The original omits health impacts (e.g., neurotoxicity, IQ reduction at ~10 ng/g), incineration risks (dioxin formation, ~0.1 ng/kg), and alternative flame retardants’ challenges (e.g., lower efficacy). - Q101: With reference to two non-conventional energy sources called 'coalbed methane' and 'shale gas', consider the following statements: - Statements: - 1. Coalbed methane is the pure methane gas extracted from coal seams, while shale gas is a mixture of propane and butane only that can be extracted from fine-grained sedimentary rocks - 2. In India, abundant coalbed methane sources exist, but so far no shale gas sources have been found - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 only - (c) Both 1 and 2 - (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Answer: - (d) Neither 1 nor 2 - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Coalbed methane (CBM) and shale gas are unconventional natural gas sources, primarily methane (CH₄), adsorbed or trapped in geological matrices. Their extraction relies on physical and chemical interactions (adsorption, diffusion). - Statement 1: CBM is ~95% CH₄, desorbed from coal seams via depressurization (Langmuir isotherm: $$ V = V_m b P / (1 + b P) $$, where $$ b \approx 0.01 \, \text{atm}^{-1} $$). Shale gas is ~70–90% CH₄, 5–10% ethane (C₂H₆), 1–5% propane (C₃H₈), and trace butane (C₄H₁₀), extracted from shale via hydraulic fracturing (fracking). Claiming shale gas is “propane and butane only” violates gas chromatography data showing methane dominance. - Statement 2: India has CBM reserves (~1.7 Tcm, e.g., Jharia, Raniganj) and shale gas potential (~2.7 Tcm, e.g., Cambay, Krishna-Godavari basins). ONGC’s shale gas pilots (e.g., Cambay, 2014) confirm recoverable resources (~0.1 Tcm extracted by 2025). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 1 mischaracterizes shale gas composition, ignoring its methane content. - (b) Incorrect: Statement 2 is false, as shale gas reserves exist in India, evidenced by geological surveys. - (c) Incorrect: Both statements are false. - (d) Correct: Neither statement holds due to compositional and geological inaccuracies. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: CBM desorption is endothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx 20 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$); fracking increases entropy by fracturing shale (porosity ~5–10%). - Quantum Considerations: CH₄’s tetrahedral structure (sp³-hybridized) minimizes intermolecular forces, aiding gas mobility. C₂–C₄ hydrocarbons have higher van der Waals interactions. - Historical Context: CBM commercialized in the USA (1980s); shale gas boomed post-2000s with fracking advances. - Experimental Methods: Gas composition via GC-FID; reserves via 3D seismic surveys. Porosity via BET analysis. - Applications: CBM/shale gas for power (~30% of India’s gas), industrial fuel. Reduces import dependency (~50% of India’s gas imported). - Global Perspective: USA leads shale gas (~20 Tcm); India’s CBM ~10% of gas production. Global shale gas ~200 Tcm. - Limitations: The original omits fracking’s environmental risks (e.g., groundwater contamination, ~1% wells leak CH₄), seismic concerns, and low shale gas yields in India due to complex geology. - Q102: Consider the following pairs: - Commonly used/consumed materials vs Unwanted or controversial chemicals likely to be found in them: - 1. Lipstick: Lead - 2. Soft drinks: Brominated vegetable oils - 3. Chinese fast food: Monosodium glutamate - Options: - (a) 1 only - (b) 2 and 3 only - (c) 1 and 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Trace contaminants in consumer products arise from raw materials or processing, raising toxicity concerns due to bioaccumulation or physiological effects. - Pair 1 (Lipstick: Lead): Lead (Pb, ~0.1–10 ppm) in pigments (e.g., PbCrO₄) inhibits enzymes (e.g., ALAD, $$ K_i \approx 10^{-6} \, \text{M} $$), causing neurotoxicity (LD₅₀ ~5 mg/kg). Pb’s d¹⁰ configuration enhances protein binding. - Pair 2 (Soft drinks: BVOs): Brominated vegetable oils (~15 ppm in citrus drinks) stabilize emulsions via high density (~1.3 g/cm³). Br accumulation (half-life ~30 days) disrupts thyroid function (e.g., T3/T4 inhibition). - Pair 3 (Chinese fast food: MSG): Monosodium glutamate (NaC₅H₈NO₄, ~0.1–1%) enhances umami via glutamate receptors (mGluR, $$ K_d \approx 10^{-5} \, \text{M} $$). Alleged “Chinese Restaurant Syndrome” (headaches, ~1% prevalence) is largely debunked (FDA GRAS status). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Excludes valid pairs 2 and 3, which have documented chemical presence. - (b) Incorrect: Excludes pair 1, where Pb is a known contaminant. - (c) Incorrect: Excludes pair 2, where BVOs are used in some drinks. - (d) Correct: All pairs correctly identify chemicals in their respective products, supported by analytical data. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Pb’s low solubility (PbCO₃, $$ K_{sp} \approx 10^{-13} $$) enhances persistence; BVOs’ C-Br bonds (~290 kJ/mol) resist metabolism. - Quantum Considerations: Pb’s heavy-metal electron configuration (6s²6p²) promotes enzyme binding; MSG’s zwitterionic structure enhances solubility. - Historical Context: Pb in cosmetics regulated since 2000s (FDA: 10 ppm); BVOs banned in EU (2014); MSG safety confirmed by WHO (1987). - Experimental Methods: Pb via ICP-MS (LOD ~0.01 ppm); BVOs via GC-MS; MSG via HPLC. Toxicity via LD₅₀ assays. - Applications: Safer alternatives (e.g., TiO₂ pigments, sucrose acetate for BVOs). MSG used in ~2 Mt/year globally. - Global Perspective: ~50% of lipsticks exceed Pb limits in developing nations; BVOs restricted in EU, Japan; MSG consumption high in Asia (~60% of global use). - Limitations: The original omits Pb’s chronic effects (e.g., IQ reduction at ~10 μg/dL), BVO alternatives’ efficacy, and MSG’s cultural perception biases. - Q103: In the context of which one of the following are the terms pyrolysis and plasma gasification mentioned? - Options: - (a) Extraction of earth element - (b) Natural gas extractions technologies - (c) Hydrogen fuel-based automobiles - (d) Waste to energy technologies - Answer: - (d) Waste to energy technologies - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Pyrolysis and plasma gasification are thermochemical processes converting organic waste into energy-rich products, driven by bond cleavage and radical recombination. - Option (a): Rare earth extraction uses hydrometallurgy (e.g., leaching with H₂SO₄), not thermal decomposition. - Option (b): Natural gas extraction involves drilling/fracking, unrelated to pyrolysis or plasma processes. - Option (c): H₂ fuel vehicles use fuel cells or combustion, not waste-derived syngas. - Option (d): Pyrolysis (400–800°C, anoxic) decomposes organics: - $$ \text{C}_n\text{H}_m\text{O}_k \rightarrow \text{syngas (CO, H}_2\text{)} + \text{bio-oil} + \text{char} $$, ~50% syngas yield (~20 MJ/kg). - Plasma gasification (~5000°C, ionized gas) converts waste to syngas and vitrified slag, with ~90% volume reduction. - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: No thermochemical basis for rare earth extraction. - (b) Incorrect: Gas extraction relies on geological processes, not thermal conversion. - (c) Incorrect: H₂ vehicles use pure H₂, not waste-derived syngas. - (d) Correct: Both processes convert waste to energy, aligning with their thermochemical mechanisms. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Pyrolysis is endothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx 100 \, \text{kJ/kg} $$); plasma gasification’s high temperature minimizes Gibbs free energy, maximizing syngas yield. - Quantum Considerations: C-C/C-H bond cleavage in pyrolysis involves homolytic fission; plasma’s ionized state enhances molecular dissociation. - Historical Context: Pyrolysis scaled in the 1990s for waste management; plasma gasification emerged in the 2000s for high-efficiency conversion. - Experimental Methods: Syngas via GC-MS (CO ~50%, H₂ ~30%); slag via XRD. Energy output via calorimetry (~1 MW/ton waste). - Applications: Syngas for power generation; char for soil amendment; slag for construction. - Global Perspective: ~100 pyrolysis plants globally; ~10 plasma plants due to high costs (~$1M/MW). India explores both for MSW (~150 Mt/year). - Limitations: The original omits energy intensity (~1 kWh/kg for plasma), dioxin risks in pyrolysis (~0.1 ng/kg), and scalability issues (high CAPEX, ~$10M/plant). - Q104: In India, the use of carbofuran, methyl parathion, phorate and triazophos is viewed with apprehension. These chemicals are used as: - Options: - (a) Pesticides in agriculture - (b) Preservatives in processed foods - (c) Fruit-ripening agents - (d) Moisturising agents in cosmetics - Answer: - (a) Pesticides in agriculture - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Carbofuran, methyl parathion, phorate (organophosphates), and triazophos (organophosphorothioate) are neurotoxic pesticides targeting acetylcholinesterase (AChE): - $$ \text{R-OP} + \text{AChE} \rightarrow \text{phosphorylated AChE} $$, $$ K_i \approx 10^{-9} \, \text{M} $$. - This inhibits acetylcholine hydrolysis, causing neural overstimulation (LD₅₀: carbofuran ~8 mg/kg, phorate ~2 mg/kg). - Option (a): These chemicals are applied to crops (e.g., rice, cotton) to control pests, leveraging their electrophilic P=O/P=S groups. - Option (b): Food preservatives (e.g., sodium benzoate, $$ \text{pK}_a \approx 4.2 $$) inhibit microbial growth, not neural function. - Option (c): Fruit ripening uses ethylene (C₂H₄) or calcium carbide, not organophosphates. - Option (d): Moisturizers use emollients (e.g., glycerin), not neurotoxins. - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Correct: Molecular structure and toxicity align with pesticide function. - (b) Incorrect: Preservatives lack AChE inhibition; chemically distinct. - (c) Incorrect: Ripening agents promote hormonal responses, not pest control. - (d) Incorrect: Moisturizers are non-toxic, unlike organophosphates. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Phosphorylation is exothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx -50 \, \text{kJ/mol} $$), irreversibly binding AChE. Persistence (half-life ~30–60 days) enhances environmental risk. - Quantum Considerations: P=O’s polarity (electronegativity: P 2.19, O 3.44) drives nucleophilic attack on AChE’s serine residue. - Historical Context: Organophosphates surged during the Green Revolution (1960s); bans (e.g., methyl parathion, 2018) reflect toxicity concerns. - Experimental Methods: Residue via LC-MS (LOD ~0.01 ppm); toxicity via LD₅₀ assays. AChE activity via Ellman assay. - Applications: Crop protection (~50% yield increase); alternatives include biopesticides (e.g., neem). - Global Perspective: India uses ~60 kt/year pesticides; ~20% organophosphates. Global bans (e.g., EU) drive safer options. - Limitations: The original omits ecological impacts (e.g., pollinator decline, ~30%), human exposure (e.g., ~10⁴ poisoning cases/year in India), and biopesticide scalability (~5% of market). - Q105: With reference to radioisotope thermoelectric generators (RTGS), consider the following statements: - Statements: - 1. RTGS are miniature fission reactors - 2. RTGS are used for powering the onboard systems of spacecrafts - 3. RTGS can use Plutonium-238, which is a by-product of weapons development - Options: - (a) 1 and 2 only - (b) 2 and 3 only - (c) 1 and 3 only - (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: - (b) 2 and 3 only - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - RTGs convert heat from radioactive decay into electricity via the Seebeck effect (thermocouple voltage: $$ V = \alpha \Delta T $$, $$ \alpha \approx 100 \, \mu\text{V/K} $$). - Statement 1: RTGs use alpha decay of Pu-238 ($$ t_{1/2} \approx 87.7 \, \text{years} $$, $$ E \approx 5.5 \, \text{MeV} $$), producing ~0.5 W/g. Fission reactors split heavy nuclei (e.g., ²³⁵U, $$ E \approx 200 \, \text{MeV/fission} $$), requiring critical mass and moderators, unlike RTGs’ subcritical decay. - Statement 2: RTGs power spacecraft (e.g., Voyager: ~470 W initial; Curiosity: ~110 W) in low-solar environments, leveraging Pu-238’s long half-life and reliability. - Statement 3: Pu-238 is produced via neutron capture: $$ \text{Np-237} + n \rightarrow \text{Pu-238} $$, often in reactors linked to weapons programs (e.g., Savannah River, 1960s). - Reasoning for Options: - (a) Incorrect: Statement 1 is false, as RTGs use decay, not fission. - (b) Correct: Statements 2 and 3 align with RTG function and Pu-238’s origin. - (c) Incorrect: Statement 1 is false. - (d) Incorrect: Includes false statement 1. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Alpha decay is exothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx 5.5 \, \text{MeV/atom} $$); thermocouple efficiency ~5%, limited by Carnot constraints. - Quantum Considerations: Pu-238’s alpha emission involves quantum tunneling; thermocouples rely on band-gap differences (e.g., Bi₂Te₃). - Historical Context: RTGs used since 1961 (SNAP-3); Pu-238 production declined post-Cold War. - Experimental Methods: Power via calorimetry; Pu-238 via gamma spectroscopy (LOD ~1 Bq). Safety via radiation shielding tests. - Applications: Deep-space missions (e.g., Voyager, 50+ years operation); Mars rovers. - Global Perspective: USA produces ~1.5 kg Pu-238/year; India explores Am-241 RTGs. Global supply ~35 kg. - Limitations: The original omits safety risks (e.g., launch failures, Pu release, ~1 Ci/kg), Pu-238 scarcity (~$8M/kg), and alternative isotopes (e.g., Sr-90). - Q106: Consider the following: - Statements: - 1. Battery storage - 2. Biomass generators - 3. Fuel cells - 4. Rooftop solar photovoltaic units - How many of the above are considered "Distributed Energy Resources"? - Options: - (a) Only one - (b) Only two - (c) Only three - (d) All four - Answer: - (d) All four - Analysis: - Fundamental Principles: - Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) are small-scale (<10 MW), localized energy systems reducing transmission losses (~5–10%). They leverage electrochemical, thermochemical, or photovoltaic processes. - Statement 1: Battery storage (e.g., Li-ion, ~200 Wh/kg) uses electrochemical reactions: - $$ \text{LiCoO}_2 + \text{C} \leftrightarrow \text{Li}_{1-x}\text{CoO}_2 + \text{Li}_x\text{C} $$, $$ E^0 \approx 3.8 \, \text{V} $$. - Enables local energy storage/discharge for grid or off-grid use. - Statement 2: Biomass generators convert organic matter (~15 MJ/kg) via combustion/gasification: - $$ \text{C}_6\text{H}_{10}\text{O}_5 + 6\text{O}_2 \rightarrow 6\text{CO}_2 + 5\text{H}_2\text{O} $$. - Small-scale units (~1–10 MW) serve rural grids. - Statement 3: Fuel cells (e.g., H₂-based) generate electricity: - $$ 2\text{H}_2 + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow 2\text{H}_2\text{O} $$, $$ E^0 \approx 1.23 \, \text{V} $$, efficiency ~50%. - Used in microgrids or standalone systems. - Statement 4: Rooftop solar PV converts sunlight (~150–200 W/m²) via the photovoltaic effect (p-n junction, Si bandgap ~1.1 eV), producing local power. - Reasoning for Options: - (a, b, c) Incorrect: All four systems meet DER criteria, as they generate/store energy locally, reducing grid dependency. - (d) Correct: Each aligns with DER’s decentralized, small-scale nature. - Enhanced Analysis: - Thermodynamic Insights: Batteries store Gibbs free energy ($$ \Delta G = -nFE $$); biomass combustion is exothermic ($$ \Delta H \approx -15 \, \text{MJ/kg} $$); fuel cells maximize work via electrochemical pathways; PV converts photon energy to electrical work (~20% efficiency). - Quantum Considerations: PV relies on electron-hole pair generation; fuel cells involve proton conduction across membranes (e.g., Nafion). - Historical Context: DERs grew post-2000s with renewable policies (e.g., Germany’s Energiewende, 2000). India’s 2015 rooftop solar push targets 40 GW by 2030. - Experimental Methods: Battery capacity via discharge tests; biomass calorific value via bomb calorimetry; fuel cell efficiency via polarization curves; PV via I-V curves. - Applications: Microgrids (~99.9% uptime), peak shaving, rural electrification (~20% of India’s villages). - Global Perspective: DERs ~10% of global capacity (2025); USA leads battery storage (~50 GW); India’s biomass ~10 GW. - Limitations: The original omits grid integration challenges (e.g., voltage fluctuations, ~5% instability), battery recycling (~5% Li-ion recycled), and biomass emissions (CO₂ ~1 kg/kWh).
